En septembre 2000, le président de l'Université Polytechnique de Cracovie @olitechnika Krakowska) et le directeur général du Cemagref ont signé un accord- cadre de coopération entre les instituts. Le hoisième signataire était I'ENGREF. L'Institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau (IIGUD en coopération avec l'institut d'Architecture du Paysage a pleinement profité de cette chance. J'observe avec satisfaction le développement de la coopération avec le Cemagref. Dans les faits les plus importants nous citerons : o    L'Institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de I'Eau réalise la coopération scientifique avec deux centres régionaux du Cemagref à Lyon et à Aix-en- Provence dans le cadre de 6 thèmes de recherche, le trois ont été acceptés comme projets Polonium ; r    2 séminaires d'étude pour le milieu scientifique et pour les ingénieurs de terrain ont eu lieu à l'Institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau. Le premier a concerné l'implémentation de la Directive Cadre sur l'Eau de l'Union Européenne, le deuxième a concerné la construction d'application de GIS pour les digues. Les hôtes de la France étaient les conférenciers et les participants le large cercle du milieu scientifique et technique ; o Quatre chercheurs de I'Institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau ont effecfué un stage, d'un à huit mois, au Cemagref, ces stages ont été financés par le côté français ; o   Un étudiant français a effectué un stage à Politechnika Krakowska ; . Il y a eu cinq réunions du comité de pilotage de la coopération (trois à Cracovie,2àParis); o Dans le cadre du projet POLONIT M sept chercheurs d'IIGW ont effectué des visites en France et six chercheurs français ont été hôtes à Politechnika Krakowska. Pendant ces visites, les résultats de recherches communes ont été présentés en séminaires d'Instituts. En conséquence de la coopération 4 articles communs ont été publiés, les rapports scientifiques, les logiciels, les films vidéo et les manuels ont été échangés. Le thème d'une thèse en cotutelle est en hain d'être formulée et le professeur Szczçsny est rapporteur d'une thèse en France. En outre, il faut remarquer, que : o Hanna Witkowska est membre du Conseil Scientifique et Technique de Cemagref (pour le période 2001-2003) et participe deux fois par an aux réunions. o Le Cemagref a proposé la candidature de l'institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau coûlme représentant de la Pologne au réseau scientifique et technique international EurAqua. Ces propositions ont été acceptées (après [SOURCE: Page_12]

11 [SOURCE: Page_12]

l'accord du milieu scientifique en Pologne). L'Institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau est membre associé d'EurAqu^4 représenté par E.Nachlik, et après l'entrée de la Pologne dans I'IIE sera membre effectif. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Le troisième anniversaire de la signature de l'accord-cadre enhe Politechnika Krakowska et le Cemagref a donné l'occasion d'organiser un séminaire de trois jours pour présenter les résultats de la coopération entre le Cemagref et IIGW. Comme l'hôte de Politechnika Krakowsk^4 je suis heureux que le lieu de ce séminaire soit Politechnika. Il faut hautement apprécier les effets de la coopération menée depuis trois ans entre le Cemagref et l'Institut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau. Au temps de la réunification de I'Europe celaaun sens particulier. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Alors je souhaite aux deux institutions participantes au séminaire la continuation du travail commun, la satisfaction des résultats et des applications pour le profit de la gestion de l'eau dans nos deux pays. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Les résultats de I'accord cadre signé en septembre 2000 entre le Cemagref et Politechnika Krakowska ont été décrits par le Recteur de Politechnika Krakowsk^4 M. Marcin Chrzanowski. [SOURCE: Page_16]

D'une manière générale la coopération concerne les travaux menés en commun, l'échange des savoirs et des expériences dans le domaine de la recherche, de I'application, et aussi de l'éducation au sens large. Cette coopération a une grande importance pour l'école Polytechnique et en particulier pour l'Tnstitut de Génie Hydraulique et de Gestion de l'Eau. Après quelques années d'intemrption de notre dernière grande coopération dans le cadre de Tempus, nous revenons à ce ÿpe de contacts, cependant ils seront réalisés dans de nouvelles conditions. Les conditions internes et externes de notre pays déterminent de nouveaux défis pour la gestion de l'eau. Elle commence à se faire dans le cadre de la politique régionale qui s'affermit en Pologne. En même temps, en vue de l'adhésion de la Pologne à I'IIE, nous introduisons des procédures d'institutionnalisation de la politique d'eau. C'est visible par l'importance grandissante des agences Régionales de l'Eau. Les missions de ces institutions grandissent. Dans ces conditions est apparue la nécessité de développer la gestion de l'eau basée sur le savoir. Cela signifie aussi qu'il faut construire de nouveaux principes de coopération pour tous les partenaires qui réalisent la mission de la gestion de l'eau dans les régions. Ces partenaires sont principalement des institutions publiques, des entreprises privées et des universités faisant offrce principalement de consultants. L'introduction de la Directive Cadre d'UE constitue le fondement de la coopération. Elle nous impose une nouvelle vue sur la planification des missions de gestion de I'eau - précédée par le diagnostic de l'état existant. Dans ce contexte la coopération entre notre Institut, le Cemagref et I'ENGREF prend une autre signification. Elle permet d'abréger le temps d'éducation et d'améliorer I'interprétation des expériences dans ce domaine. D'autre part, notre proche coopération avec l'Agence d'Eau de Cracovie, la Direction d'Amélioration Agricole et l'Institut de Météorologie et Gestion d'Eau garantie que les expériences acquises seront largement appliquées en pratique. [SOURCE: Page_16]

Après avoir analysé notre coopération avec le Cemagref et I'ENGREF, je suis persuadée que nous n'avons pas perdu notre temps et que nous avons contribué au [SOURCE: Page_16]

15 [SOURCE: Page_16]

développement de nos jeunes cadres. D'autre part, j'espère que nos partenaires sont eux aussi satisfaits des résultats de la coopération, celle-ci leur permettant d'acquérir de nouvelles expériences. En effet la gestion de l'eau est basée sur des conditions locales qui dépendent du pays. Elles sont la conséquence de la vitesse du développement de la culture technique, des coutumes et du régime en place. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Les résultats de notre coopération présentés aujourd'hui constituent seulement une partie des effets qui peuvent être traités plus largement. Ils concernent aussi l'éducation au sens large : les améliorations de la connaissance pratique des langues par nos jeunes chercheurs, l'apprentissage de la pratique quotidienne de la réalisation des missions de la gestion d'eau dans les deux pays, mais aussi la découverte de la beauté de la France. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Je voudrais remercier nos partenaires pour la possibilité de cette coopération et l'Ambassade de France pour sa bienveillance et son aide à la coopération. [SOURCE: Page_17]

ElzbietaNachlik [SOURCE: Page_17]

Seminar opening Patrick Lavarde Directeur Général - Cemagref - France [SOURCE: Page_18]

Depuis quelques années une collaboration hipartite a été développée entre le Cemagref I'ENGREF et Politechnika Krakowska. Elle porte sur des disciplines telles que I'hydrologie et, l'hydraulique, appliquées à la prévention des crues, la gestion et la conception des ouvrages hydrauliques, la prévention et I'analyse de risques ainsi que la qualité et la gestion des milieux aquatiques continentaux. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Ainsi, en 2000, un accord-cadre de collaboration a été signé, confirmant les intérêts et souhaits réciproques de collaborer en vue de développer des programmes et des projets de recherche, ainsi que des échanges franco-polonais. Cet accord-cadre ne faisait que formaliser des collaborations déjà anciennes ou, comme souvent dans la recherche, les relations personnelles entre chercheurs qui avaient initié des travaux de qualité et ouverts la voie. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Cet accord cadre, dans le domaine de la recherche, mais aussi dans celui de la formation pour I'environnement, couvre la période de 2000 à 2005. Il s'est concrétisé par le montage de projets bilatéraux (Polonium) et multilatéraux dans le cadre du 5"'" PCRD de I'UE, et par des échanges de scientifiques (doctorants, post doctorants et chercheurs confirmés). Ces collaborations scientifiques ont abouti à des échanges de savoir-faire, d'informations et de résultats de qualité. Nous avons également, au Cemagref, le plaisir de compter Hanna Witkowska, professeur à l'université de Cracovie, parmi les personnalités scientifiques membres de notre Conseil scientifique et technique. La collaboration entre le Cemagref et Politechnika est amenée à se développer encore davantage du fait de la qualité de nos résultats communs, mais aussi de I'entrée en 2004 de Politechnika en tant que membre effectif dans le réseau européen d'organismes de recherche sur les eaux de surface EurAqua dont le Cemagref est membre fondateur. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Par ailleurs, nous unirons certainement aussi nos compétences dans le cadre du 6è-" PCRD de I'IIE, par le montage et la participation à des projets européens de recherche et par des échanges de chercheurs, en particulier en mobilisant le programme Marie Curie. Ces recherches, menées en coûlmun, serviront également de base à une meilleure application de la directive cadre européenne sur l'eau qui représente un véritable défi pour les scientifiques et les responsables des politiques publiques. Aujourd'hui, à mi parcours, nous sommes fiers de présenter les résultats et les perspectives de cette collaboration exemplaire. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Dynamic Slowing of Runoff from the Catchment [SOURCE: Page_20]

The DSD concept and its application to the mountainous lsepnica catchment Céline BOUDnRO', Barbara KOpCZyNSKA$_2$, Christine POULARDI, Jerzy SZCZçSNY', Hanna WTKOWSKA$_2$ l Hydrology-Hydraulics Research Unit, Cemagref,3bis QuaiChauveau CP 220,69336 LYON Cedex 09, France ; Conesponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] 2lnstituteofWaterEngineeringandWatérManageffiska, ul.Warszawska 24, 31 -1 55 Krakôw, Poland ; Conesponding author : [EMAIL_REDACTED] pk.edu. pl [SOURCE: Page_21]

English summary Cemagref and PK designed a full-scale project to apply the Dynamic Slowing Down concept for flood mitigation in the mountainous lsepnica catchment (south of Cracow). Field work, data collecting and interviews were performed to design the best strategy regarding the catchment's topography and features. Crosswise water channels and reservoirs in natural depressions were proposed in the hillslopes, and a dry reservoir in the main river bed. Local authorities expressed great interest for the study, and building of pilot structures is envisaged. Quantification of the influence of the proposed strategy flood mitigation is addressed by the next paper. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Résumé français : Le concept de Ralentissement Dynamique et son application au bassin ÿersant montagneux de l'Isepnica [SOURCE: Page_21]

Le Cemagref et PK ont réalisé un avant-projet grandeur nature appliquant le concept de Ralentissement Dynamique pour écrêter les crues dans le bassin de I'lsepnica (au sud de Cracovie). Des études de terrain, le recensement des données et des discussions avec les riverains ont permis de concevoir une stratégie adaptée à la topographie et aux spécificités du bassin. Des canaux transverses et des microretenues dans des dépressions naturelles ont été proposés sur les pentes, et une retenue sèche dans le lit principal. Les autorités locales sont très intéressées par cette étude, et la construction de struc{ures pilotes est envisagée. L'article suivant traite de la quantification de I'effet du projet sur l'écrêtement des crues. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Streszczenie polskie : Koncepcja zastosowania metody OpdZniania Dynamicznego dla zlewni gôrskiej potoku Isepnica [SOURCE: Page_21]

Cemagref wspôlnie z Politechnikq Krakowskq opracowaÿ kompleksowy projekt zastosowania metody Dynamicznego Opô2niania (DSD) dla zlagodzenia skutkôw powodzi w gôrskiej zlewni potoku lsepnica (na pofudnie od Krakowa). W celu wypracowania najlepszej strategii, z punktu widzenia topografii oraz zagospodarowania, wykonano prace terenowe, zebrano dane oraz przeprowadzono wywiady z mieszkaÉcami. Zaproponowano zastosowanie kanal^6w poprzecznych oraz pulapek zatorowych na stokach doliny oraz wykonanie suchego zbiomika w glôwnym korycie cieku. Lokalne wladze vtykazaÿ du^2e zainteresowanie dla prowadzonych badaÉ, przewiduje siç budowç stanowiska doéwiadczalnego. llo^6ciowa ocena wplywu proponowanej strategii na ograniczenie skutkôw powodzi zostanie przedstawiona w nastepnych referatach tej grupy temaÿcznej. [SOURCE: Page_21]

19 [SOURCE: Page_21]

lntroduction Water and aquatic habitats are better and better protected by law. The 2000 EC Water Framework Directive states in its Article 1 that "Water is not a commercial product like any other but, rather, a heritage which must be protected, defended and treated as such" and strongly stresses the importance of water bodies conservation and restoration. Policies over the past centuries often considered wetlands and floodplains as land to be conquered by men. As the land-use in the floodplain changed, floodings had to be fought, and engineers undertook many heavy hydraulic works over the centuries. This is now completely re-considered. [SOURCE: Page_22]

The first part of this paper defines the Dynamic Slowing Down concept, proposed in this context of re-asserted concern for sustainable protection and ecosystem conservation. lt aims at promoting flood mitigation strategies with as little negative impact on the environment as possible, and even with positive effects of the hydrosystem dynamics. The second part presents the lsepnica, a mountainous catchment with flood and erosion problems. Significant damages regularly occur. Local authorities show big interest in the joint research of Cemagref and Politechnika Krakowska and expect applicable guidelines to improve flood protection in that area. The third part exposes the suggestions made by Cemagref and PK to apply a DSD strategy adapted to the catchment's topography and features. [SOURCE: Page_22]

1. The Dynamic Slowing Down concept Land-use change and hydraulic works usually accelerate runoff, with negative impacts on erosion and flood peaks. ln reaction appeared the idea of favouring slowing down features to get back to a better-balanced state. This concept was developed in Cemagref Lyon (Oberlin, Gilard et al., 1997) under the name "Dynamic Slowing Down". [SOURCE: Page_22]

1.1. An integrated management strategy at the catchments scale DSD is an integrated flood management strategy at the catchment scale. Many levees or river-training were designed after'local thinking' : the problem is solved at a given place, but the flow is now diverted on the other bank or downstream. An example of tool for the catchmentecale planning is Cemagrefs lnondabilité method (Gendreau et al., 1997). lt was devised to help decision- makers and land-planners to check whether the requested levels of protection are reasonable regarding the land-use and the flood hazard at catchment-scale. "lntegrated" means that all the stakes are taken into acrount, not only floods: ecology, water quantiÿ and qualiÿ, landscape... and also economy and human activi§. [SOURCE: Page_22]

1.2. Slowing down without hampering natural dynamics DSD proposes a strategy of SLOWING DOWN, which is expected to reduce erosion and delay the time of arrival of runoff in the river. But it stresses that natural DYNAMICS must always be respected. For ecological issues and [SOURCE: Page_22]

20 [SOURCE: Page_22]

geomorphology, the main channel must be as little modified as possible, and overflows allowed. The main requirements can be expressed as a rule of "3 continuities": -   longitudinal continuiÿ: the flow, sediment and live beings must be free to move along the river, as in the natural state ; -   transverse continuiÿ: overflows to the floodplain arc a natural process, beneficial for flood mitigation downstreams, but also positive for the life cycle of many life beings, living both in the river and in the river ecosystems ; [SOURCE: Page_23]

-   vertical continuiÿ: water must have enough time to infiltrate and feed the groundwater tables. [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.3. Possible tools and techniques to implement DSD First, once the bad side effects of acceleration are demonstrated, any further acceleration must be stopped. Then, where it is possible, you can try and slow down runoff by good practises, small structures or adaptation of existing structures. For this, many tools can be used, wherever slowing down is possible and acceptable. [SOURCE: Page_23]

Figure I : opportunities for slowing down (SD) or temporary retention (TR) in a catchment [SOURCE: Page_23]

Figure 1 shows examples of slowing down features ("SD"), such as roughened strips and transverse obstacles, and temporary retention devices ("TR"). These latter are the most efficient for flood mitigation ; they are obtained by local reduction of flow capaciÿ, whether in artificial networks (smaller outlet in drainage ditches or under a road) or in natural channels (dry reservoir, Figure 2). Of course, allowing the river to overflow in its floodplain is efficient for flood mitigation downstreams, but it requires to maintain a land-use compatible with [SOURCE: Page_23]

21 [SOURCE: Page_23]

these overflows. Therefore, work has been undertaken to enhance the effects of this floodplain on the flood peaks by roughened strips, transverse obstacles and dry reservoirs (Ghavasieh, 2003). [SOURCE: Page_24]

Dry reservoirs are dams with an opening at main channel location (Figure 2). \Mren necessary precautions are taken during conception stage, they will not hinder water, sediment or animals circulation during low and medium flows (1). Only when incoming discharges get superior to the outlet capaciÿ will the flow be modified (2). They make it possible to maintain outflow under a given value. When the reservoir behind the dam is filled up, the flow is evacuated by an overflow to prevent damages to the structure (3). Paper 3 will address dry reservoirs optimisation. [SOURCE: Page_24]

### 2. DSD Application to the Mountains Catchment

2.1. Selection of the catchment So far, not enough results coming from the DSD method implementation exist. ln France, research and experimental works were done mainly on the plain rivers. ln Poland, the mountainous catchment was selected for study. Such a choice allows [SOURCE: Page_24]

22 [SOURCE: Page_24]

us to test the method efficiency in different condition of the runoff and undoubtedly contribute to the development of computational tools. Politechnika Krakowska chose for tests the catchment of the lsepnica Torrent, right tributary of the Sola River. lt is located south of Cracow, within the area of the Czernichow commune and it is characteristic for the mountains region in Poland. (Figure^3). [SOURCE: Page_25]

## POLAND

Figure 3 : Location of the lsepnica catchment ln the lsepnica river bed, many hydraulic structures were built for its stabilisation. Despite of that, during floods, significant damages regularly occur. Therefore, it is necessary to find additional methods for flood protection, which will limit flood damages in combination with the existing ones. The choice of the Isepnica catchment can be additionally justified by their neighbouring location to the experimental catchment of Politechnika Krakowska (the \Melka Puszcza Tonent catchment            - Figure 4). There, since many years hydrologic measurements have been done. This catchment is similar to lsepnica in physiographical features, which allows transposition hydrologic data from one catchment to another. Vÿielka Puszcza catchment is less developed as yet and it has not such significant flood loss and flood protection needs. [SOURCE: Page_25]

It is very important that local authorities show big interest in the joint research of Cemagref and Politechnika Krakowska and they expect applicable guidelines to improve flood protection in that area. [SOURCE: Page_25]

2.2. Available data For the implementation of the DSD method, the first stage is to prepare the most exact morphological and area development data as well as hydrologic ones. Some of those data come from literature or existing documentation. But the selection of RD structures, i.e. their location, ÿpes and structural materials is specific for each place and can not be only based even on detailed maps. lt requires field visits, very detailed area examination and its identification. For the lsepnica catchment the existing documentation was gathered: maps (scale 1:1000, 1:10000), land development plans, geological and hydrological information. The local administration and people were interviewed. Further on, the fleld works were performed: identification of problems in the catchment (Figure 5) and inventory of their geomorphologic conditions. ïhe main riverbed and all hydraulic structures existing in it were described and plotted on the map too. A photographic documentation in different periods was assembled (Figures 6 and 7). [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure 5 : lsepnica catchment land development 2.3. Description of the lsepnica catchment and the main river The lsepnica catchment is located in Beskid Maly (the hÇhest mountain Jawozyna 861 m a.s.l.). The surface of the catchment is 7.8 km'. The maximum length of the catchment is 4.7 km, and its width is about 1.7 km. Slopes are cut by streams. Average slope is 18o to 19o on the left side of the stream and 10o to 13o on the right one. Average elementary runoff from 1 km^2 is about 40 l/s. Area of the catchment is built with sandstone with thin layers of clay stones. The whole thickness of the complex is about 500 m. The bottom of the valley is covered with alluvial soil (sediments) and weathered clay which are the effect of sloping process. There are also landslides areas (Figure 5). \Mthin the catchment area there are two settlements : Miçdzybrodzie Zywieckie and Mjiçdzybrodzie Zar. They are located along the local road which leads to the upper reservoir of the pumped-storage power plant on the Zar mountain (the border of the catchment). Buildings are scattered, except in the lower part of the valley which is the area of the lsepnica mouth were buildings are concentrated mainly on the right bank. [SOURCE: Page_27]

25 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Figure 7 : Examples of damages in the main river bed after flood 2001 [SOURCE: Page_28]

26 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Afforestation of the catchment is 62%. The forest is partly exploited and partly belongs to the Beskid Maly Landscape Park. Concerning morphology of the area, favourable conditions to the agricultural production are on the gentler slopes and in the lower part of the catchment, on the north from lsepnica mouth. The region has large significance in tourism. ln plans of local administration, agrotourism is the main target. The lsepnica Stream begins below the Kiczera mountain, about 800 m a.s.l. and flows to the Sola River on the height of 329 m a.s.l. in the part which is at the same time the bacl<water of the Porqbka water reservoir. Main river gradient is from 36% in the source part to 5% in the mouth part. ln the main river bed some hydraulic structures were constructed. There are two sediment dams constructed for limitation the sediment transport to the Por^4bka water reservoir. There are also some smaller structures built for river banks flood protection. They were constructed by forest service and local people and they are mainly not documented. ln the river bed, in the part between two mentioned above sediment dams, there is a structure which seems to be the dam of dry reservoir. The bowl of the reservoir is completely filled up. As a result of erosion, downstream part of the dam is almost completely destroyed and may cause destruction of the dam. The stream is in an unstable state. Every flood causes not only erosion of the banks and in the main river bed but also in small torrents, where as the result of forest works (construction of forest tracks with steep slopes, hauling felled trees along the stream course, etc.) the equilibrium is disturbed. The sediments dams stops sediments indistinctly (75% of suspended loads) and this causes also strong erosion downstream. Those dams, despite their significant height were filled up in a short time. At present, after every flood, deposits from reservoirs bowl are partly removed to make room for sediments of the next flood. All hydraulic structures need renovation. [SOURCE: Page_29]

2.4. Strategy and designs of structures After detailed analysis of existing documentation and collected data, and after several field visits within the lsepnica catchment, the strategy of decreasing the slope runoff and limitation of erosion using different RD structures was proposed. The RD concept concerned the whole catchment and included the proposal of structure built on the slopes (trenches, water traps), adaptation of forest tracks and construction of a dry reservoir in the main river bed. Figure 8 displays the proposed structures location. At the first stage, only hydraulic and geotechnical aspects were considered. Secondly, the properÿ and land-use limitations will be taken into account. On the slopes, utilization of the existing or future forest tracks is proposed. ln this way not only their role in the runoff acceleration will be limited but also slowing down the runoff by dispersion will be achieved. For the second class tonents, two different ÿpes of structures are suggested : .   crosswise water channels to lengthen the ways of flood flow, [SOURCE: Page_29]

27 [SOURCE: Page_29]

development of natural depressions which after proper adaptation could be some kind of dry reservoirs filling up during the flood; if such natural depression are not located on the river course, the system of channels for transfer the excess water coming from runoff or the stream must be proposed. [SOURCE: Page_30]

ln the main river bed, the construction of a dry reservoir in two possible locations of the dam cross-section was presented. ln the end, the construction of special traps for stopping some elements canied by water was proposed. Those traps let water to flow through but they fully or partly stop floating elements. They are supposed to stop carried material in places appointed in advance. [SOURCE: Page_30]

2.5. Discussion Techniques are expected to be of low cost, feasible, and with little negative impact on the environment. However, they required a thorough examination of [SOURCE: Page_30]

28 [SOURCE: Page_30]

topography, and specific problems arise for assessment. lndeed, usual hydraulic tools are not suitable to model a set if works distributed throughout the catchment, and hydrological data on the catchment are so far too scarce. The authors nevertheless developed an innovative approach to assess the effect of the proposed set of works on flood mitigation. Two other PK-Cemagref conference papers present the following stages. A second paper focuses on development of special computation tools: a hydrological distributed model was specifically developed to model the effect of small hydraulic works in the slopes runoff. lt was coupled to a hydraulic model better suited for flow in main river bed, to which it gives the results of its slope runoff calculations as input hydrographs. This also made it possible to test different scenarios, to compare the effect of a generalized slowing down to the effect of a slowing down limited to some slopes, in order to de-synchronise the arrival of the slopes contributions at the outlet. Then, paper 3 addresses dry reservoir parameters dimensioning, using flood scenarios estimated for given return periods. [SOURCE: Page_31]

3.Conclusion and Percpectives Two main targets were defined for this stage of the study. First of all it was preparing the most exact data set (morphological, area development, hydrological, etc.) and building an RD strategy on the catchment, which is the topic of this paper. This data base were used later for quantitative assessment of efficiency of the proposed strategy for erosion limitation and reduction of flood loss in the lsepnica catchment. However, other practical issues must also be considered in more advanced stages: .   infiltration: how quickly will the hillslope depressions empÿ by infiltration ? Can this additional infiltration worsen landslides ? .   overflows acceptability : are the proposed overflow locations suitable ? Can the new water path cause damages during flood events, because of water velociÿ or sediment deposits risks or poor water qualiÿ ? Are they compatible with land-use ? Wll they be accepted by the land- owners ? .   maintenance: who will be in charge of the regular and after-flood checks ? what kind of works can be required and at which cost (sediment removal, levees rei nforcement... ). [SOURCE: Page_31]

As the result of our works we plan to prepare applicable guidelines and operational proposals to improve flood protection in the lsepnica catchment. They are expected to be helpful in planning and management of the flood protection in that area, and also to give hints and methodological guidance for application in other cases. [SOURCE: Page_31]

The choice of an outlet for lspenica Dry Reseruoir Anna LENAR-MAWASI, Christine. pOUleRO$_2$, Jezy SZCZESNY', Hanna WTKOWSKA' 1 lnstitute of Water Engineering and Water Management, Cracow Universiÿ of Technology, Poland i Corresponding author : [EMAIL_REDACTED] 2Hydrology-Hydrautics"Resear@hauveaucP22o,69336 LYON Cedex 09, France ; Corresponding author : [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_33]

English summâry Dry reservoirs are one of the Dynamic Slowing Down techniques applicable in mountainous areas. They can efficiently mitigate floods, with as little negative effects as possible on the hydrosystem. This paper focuses on the design and dimensionning of a dry reservoir proposed for lsepnica. We show how the dimensions of the sluice can be adjusted to optimize its behaviour for a design hydrograph. However, our work confirms the importance of the hydrological analysis. lndeed, we found a one-to-two factor between 100-year return period peak discharges estimated by two different methods. We also showed that two successive S$_0$-year floods are equivalent to one 1O$_0$-year flood downstream the dam. [SOURCE: Page_33]

Résumé français z Dimensionnement du pertuis de la retenue sèche de l'Isepnica [SOURCE: Page_33]

Les retenues sèches sont une technique de Ralentissement Dynamique applicable en milieu de relief. Elles sont efficaces pour laminer les crues, avec peu d'effets négatifs sur I'hydrosystème. Cet article traite de la conception et du dimensionnement d'une retenue sèche proposée pour I'lsepnica. Nous montrons comment les dimensions du pertuis peuvent être ajustées pour optimiser son fonctionnement pour la crue de projet. Cependant, l'étape déterminante est l'analyse hydrologique. En effet, deux méthodes différentes pour estimer I'hydrogramme centennal ont conduit à des débits de pointe allant du simple au double. De même, deux crues cinquantennales successives reviennent à une crue centennale à l'aval du barrage. [SOURCE: Page_33]

lntroduction [SOURCE: Page_34]

Dry reservoirs are one of the possible Dynamic Slowing Down techniques applicable in mountainous areas. These techniques are not means a new idea: some are over one century old. Located in main river beds, they are efficient on flood mitigation, and were found to be a good complement to hillslope structures. This paper will address the questions of their design for hydraulic efficiency, adjusted here by adapting the outlet dimensions, and of their stabiliÿ. ln addition to these criteria, their design must also respect some ecological requirements to become truly an effective DSD tool with little or no negative effect on the environment. [SOURCE: Page_34]

1. Existing dry reservoirs The only objective of the dry reservoirs is flood wave mitigation. They are composed of a dam blocking the floodplain, but with an opening for the main channel (Fig. 1). During the low and average flows all the water flows through the sluice, no water is stored - hence the name "dry" reservoir. Thus, the bowl can be used for agriculture because it will be flooded only very occasionally - once every 10 or 100 years, depending on the project design. [SOURCE: Page_34]

tn the beginning of the XXh century in today Poland (Sudeÿ Mountains), 13 dry reservoirs were constructed, all in the Oder water basin      - on Bober, Kaczawa rivers and their tributaries, and in watershed of Nysa Klodzka. These dry reservoirs represent small storage reservoirs mostly built in mountainous areas in the upper course of mountain torrents. Their catchment is from few to few hundreds square km. As seen from the preserved design documentation, previously the dams were - built with automatic outlets (spillways and orifices) the maximal outflow was determined by the outlet devices dimensions. Later on, in the XXth century (1925- 30) in order to control the outflow manually-controlled valves were installed. The two reservoirs Buk^6wka on Bober River and Slup on Nysa Szalona River were turned into storage reservoirs with constant head. The dams are mostly made of earth with concrete outlet structures. Only dams of Zarek and Miçdzygôze Reservoirs were constructed from stones with cement mortar. The heights of dams are from 4 lo 20 meters, and the storage capaciÿ varies lrom 0,74 to 6,0 millions cubic meters, the inundated area is from 7 to 214 hectares. Detailed information concerning the reservoirs and dams are presented in Table 1. [SOURCE: Page_34]

Recent constructions Én be now found in France, like in Roanne (Fig. 1b) or Ceyrac-Conqueyrac, and many more are under study (on the Huisne river upstream of Le Mans, in Millau...). [SOURCE: Page_34]

2. Proposition of technicat solution To make a realistic proposition for the lsepnica, the following requirements must be met: -        low cost, which can be achieved by locating the structure in a narrow section, and choosing local materialto build the dam ; -       the upstream part, forming the bowl, must be large enough to let the structure store a sufficient volume, and its flooding must be accepted by the land owner; -       reliability, and in particular non-controlled functioning: located in a small catchment, with no technical services dedicated to its maintenance, the structure must not use complex devices for fear they could be blocked during a flood ; -       not disturbing for low and medium flows ; -       no additionalsedimentation behind the dam ; -       the outlet must withstand under-pressure flow; -       when the upstream bowl is full, the dam or a specifically designed weir and spillway must withstand overflow        , [SOURCE: Page_35]

-       the outlet must allow invertebrates and fish movement from downstream to upstream and vice-versa, in particular, a long and dark outlet is an obstacle for most fish ; -       the dam must be well-accepted in the landscape. [SOURCE: Page_35]

34 [SOURCE: Page_36]

3. Computations Once the principles are set, the dam must be designed to meet both hydraulic and ecologic requirements. This paper will mainly address hydraulic issues. The first step consists in fixing the requirements. Here, the reservoir is expected to mitigate the one-hundred return period flood. More frequent floods are expected to be mitigated well-enough by hillslope strucutres, and pose no threat to the village. To optimise flood mitigation, computations must be canied out to check that the sluice is: - large enough to let medium floods pass ; the reservoir must be empty while there is no need for outflow reduction - small enough to limit the design hydrograph(s) as wished. Here, we made our computations with only one dry reservoirs, no hillslope works are taken into account. [SOURCE: Page_37]

3.1. Required data To calculate the transformation of the flood wave by dry reservoir the following data are necessary : . lnflow hydrograph ; . Reservoir capaciÿ curve ; o Characteristics of outflow devices: orifice and spillway. Hydraulic tools The calculations can be carried out either by mathematical model of Saint-Venant equations or by simple hydraulic computations. These latter consist in successive computations for fine time-steps, for each step the volume and depth of corresponding layer is determined, the outflow from outlets is calculated. The following stages of the outfrow can be determined (Figure 3) : . the first when the orifice is not filled by waterthe outflow is then calculated as open channel flow when there is no contraction, or in the case of contraction as broad crested weir; . the second when the water head is slightly higher than orifice edge - then cavitation appears, perturbing the outflow (Figure 3 : the curve seems to go backwards) ; . the third when the water head is important and the outflow could be calculated as underpressure outflow, using formulae depending on the outlet shape and length ; . the fourth when the emergency spillway starts to work. [SOURCE: Page_37]

Comparing 1% flood waves obtained through 2 approaches ln small mountainous catchments, as studied here, the maximal flood waves are caused by storm rains or long duration rains. The storm waves give maximal outflows but not maximal flood wave volume, which occurs during the rains of long duration. Therefore the capaciÿ of the dry reservoir should be calculated for the most unfavourable conditions, that is for high and long-lasting flow. [SOURCE: Page_38]

The first computations were carried out using as input a hydrograph obtained through discharge regionalization, using Punzet formula (Q is calculated as function of catchment surface, torrent length, difference of altitudes, slope, mean annual precipitation, and land cover indexes). The simplified approach and the St- Venant model for the lsepnica river, using RubarBe' gave similar results. Figure 4 displays RubarBe results, which show a satisfactory mitigation, under 8 m^3/s. But an other approach was also used to estimate lhe 1% probabiliÿ flood on the lsepnica. As presented in the previous paper, a rainfall-runoff modelwas designed for the lsepnica catchment, Roof&Pipe Using as input a 1% probabiliÿ rainfall, also estimated by a regional formula (Blaszczyk's formula), the model estimates at the dry reservoir abscissa a much higher hydrograph (Fig 5). -   Blaszczyk formula (it was creaeted only for area of Poland): .(r' ."Y J- 6,631 1o,az J [Us'ha] - intensiÿ of rain H [mm] annual precitipation for lsepnica H = 750 mm it is for altitude of - crossection of dam, C - 1O$_0$/probability of rain for example 100l^10o/o t[minutes] - time of pracitipation [SOURCE: Page_38]

As we already stressed, designing the input hydrograph is a very crucial step, on which the all the following results depend. Though, it is a most delicate stage, especially when so little data are available. But even if we had a good knowledge of the flood regime, a single hydrograph is not always sufficient. lndeed, with one single hydrograph it is assumed that the reservoir is empÿ at the beginning of the flood, which is the most favourable case. Figure 7 shows that two successive 2% probabiliÿ floods are of course more dangerous than one, and they are almost as dangerous as one 1% probabiliÿ flood (Figure 6b). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Michel (1987,1989) advises not to run one isolated design hydrograph, but if possible a whole discharge time series, to take successive floods into account when estimating a mitigated regime. [SOURCE: Page_40]

38 [SOURCE: Page_40]

4. Conclusions Our work emphasises the importance of the protection objectives definition and design hydrographs estimation. lf the hydrological analysis is not made thoroughly, all subsequent results are questionable. A set of possible hydrographs, from optimistic to pessimistic options and with different durations, should be tested to take uncertainÿ into account. This is even more important when little data is available, and thus uncertainÿ on the design hydrograph is huge, like in the lsepnica case. Besides, the assessment is often carried out on one design hydrograph; but it is interesting to check effect with a set of successive hydrographs, or -even better- with a whole time-series. The dry reservoir designed by us takes into account fish needs, and in particular the choice of a thin wall was made in order not to create a dark passage. The risk of floating material clogging the sluice is also to be addressed during following stages. On the whole the dry reservoir was proved here to be efficient for 100-year return period floods. Moreover, as shown in the previous papers, it is proposed to build it altogether with hillslope works, which will enhance flood mitigation. Both ÿpes of structures are complementary: hillslope works are very efficient on moderate and medium floods and reduce erosion, whereas the dry reservoir only interferes with rare floods. [SOURCE: Page_41]

English summâry Simplified distributed parameter models, OneSecond and Roof&Pipe, were developed specifically for the preliminary studies of different Dynamic Slowing Down scenarios in the mountainous lsepnica catchment. The computation results show the effectiveness of DSD structures in the catchment. The linkage between hydrological and hydraulic models offers many interesting perspectives. We introduced lateral hydrographs computed by Roof&Pipe as input into a St-Venant model of the main river. This model, RubarBe, was useful to check Roof&Pipe's results, to build scenarios with heterogeneous rainfall, to model structures that the hydrological models can not handle -like dry reservoirs-, and helps to locate hydraulic jumps which threaten river bed stabiliÿ. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Résumé français ; Modèles Mathématiques pour les techniques de Ralentissement Dynamique dans des bassins ÿersants de montagne [SOURCE: Page_42]

Deux modèles hydrologiques distribués simplifiés, OneSecond et Roof&Pipe, ont été développés spécifiquement pour prouver I'efficacité des scénarios d'aménagements de Ralentissement Dynamique pour laminer les crues du bassin versant montagneux de l'lsepnica. Leur utilisation conjointe avec un modèle hydraulique ouvre des perspectives intéressantes. La rivière principale a été modélisée avec un logiciel hydraulique basé sur St- Venant, Rubarbe, les apports latéraux étant calculés par Roof&Pipe. Ceci a permis de vérifier les résultats de Roof&Pipe à I'aval, de tester des scénarios avec pluie hétérogène, d'introduire une retenue sèche, el enfin de localiser les ressauts hydrauliques qui menacent la stabilité du lit. [SOURCE: Page_42]

### 1. Characteristics of mountainous catchment:

Mountain catchments are characterized by high slope and morphologically varied land surface. The stream beds have often gully form and are accompanied by a network of talwegs which form small torrents during the heavy rains. The catchment area is covered by forests, meadows, waste lands and small cultivated agriculture fields. ln the slopes are located forest roads and haulage paths for timber exploitation. The precipitation has very often storm rains character. The example of such mountainous catchment management was presented in the previous paper. That management could slow down the runoff, limit the land erosion, protect slopes against slides and restore water conditions in the soil and in consequence diminish the peak discharges in the river valleys. [SOURCE: Page_43]

2. The DSD management The principles of DSD and its application to the main valleys were described in the previous paper. ln the present paper the system methodology of the mountainous catchment development is outlined. [SOURCE: Page_43]

Cascade of water traps Cross-section                              Plan ÿew [SOURCE: Page_43]

rll Jll [SOURCE: Page_43]

Modified road Cross-section                              Plan view [SOURCE: Page_43]

Fig.l. Schemes of small hydraulic structures The chosen management of the mountainous catchment consists in small hydraulic structures like: water traps, remodeled forest roads, derivation trenches and waterdistribution trenches. They are presented in a schematicway in Fig.1. The construction of forest roads, with the inclination opposite to the slope, permits to concentrate the outflow in the trenches, and distribute water into areas free of [SOURCE: Page_43]

41 [SOURCE: Page_43]

erosion risk or to direct it to the water traps. ln Fig.2 the principles of the small structures work are shown in a schematic way. Water traps are located in topographically convenient places and collect water from their sub-catchments and from transferring trenches. The modified forest roads have reverse transversal slopes, trenches situated at the hillside with sediment collectors and culverts distributing flow to the traps or safe areas. [SOURCE: Page_44]

2.1 Models used in the study The objective of the present study was to calculate the effect of the small structures on the runoff from the catchment and the flood mitigation in the main torrent. Therefore, for the dimensioning the most disadvantageous conditions in the catchment were taken under consideration. An assumption of completely saturated catchment and intensive storm ÿpe of precipitation was taken for the computations. ln order to calculate the effects of the applied structures and to determine their dimensions, a specific approach to the runoff modeling is needed. lt has to be mentioned that in the most small mountainous catchment there is no measured data, not only for surface runoff, but even for the local precipitation. Therefore are necessary indepth analyses: of: existing empiricalformulae, hydrological methods of rainfall data extension, catchment runoff coefficients and surface roughness,. The choice of the model was directed by technical demands - the most hazardous conditions and relatively simple way of computation. The review of existing hydrological models [8, 10] for the surface runoff showed that it was difficult to find one fully adequate to our assumptions and to the emplacement of the small structures in the morphologically diverse mountainous catchment. There exist very developed hydrological models taking into account many important factors as evapotranspiration and infiltration, like for example WISTOO model, but it requires numerous data and is too sophisticated for preliminary calculations. That is why new mathematical models, called OneSecond and Roof&Pipe, were developed [7, 91, which are a compromise between robustness and accuracy. Both models [SOURCE: Page_44]

42 [SOURCE: Page_44]

belong to the "gray box" ÿpe; the elementary units are described in a simplified way without mathematical description of the physical process. From the hydrological models classification the both models are distributed parameters models. The choice of the models was justified by a scarciÿ of exact data, easy introduction of structures and the relatively easy operation. Therefore the choice of scenarios could be done on base of these models for the preliminary study. For the designing purposes more advanced model should be used. [SOURCE: Page_45]

Fig. 3. Computational grid and Iocation of DSD structures ln both models the first division of the catchment is based on it morphological characteristic (Fig. 3.), the catchment is divided into a grid which well represents the varied topography and allows to introduce the small structures The difference between models consists on the second order division. ln OneSecond the first order surfaces are divided into elementary surfaces which length corresponds to the distance covered by the flow during one second ; therefore the second order division depends on the intensity of precipitation, moreover the elementary units are of irregular forms corresponding to surface relief. The Roof&Pipe model is based on the first order division (there exists the possibiliÿ of more dense grid). [SOURCE: Page_45]

43 [SOURCE: Page_45]

The Roof&Pipe model has an advantage of quick computations (10 minutes one run) and very easy introduction of new management scenarios. The OneSecond represents more exactly the physical nature of the runoff. The examples of the computationalgrid are presented in Fig.4. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Table I The results of computations by mathematical models. ln order to obtain the most dangerous conditions for the work of hydraulic structures, a somehow artificial hydrological situation was taken into consideration: very low runoff coefficients (not taking into account varied relief of small surfaces, heavy rain of relatively long duration, completely saturated catchment). The computations of the hydrographs at the mouth of each tributary and lateral discharge for the existing catchment were performed by OneSecond and Roof&Pipe models. 3. Combining hydrologic models output and hydraulic modelling To benefit from the distributed information of R&P as well as from the accuracy of the St-Venant-based model Rubar in the main channel, it was decided to link both : - R&P is used to compute the runoff on all the subcatchments ; - outflow from tributary and hillslope elements towards lsepnica are then used as input in RubarBe. [SOURCE: Page_50]

1. Adapting RubarBe to receive the intermediate computation from R&P as input. The input to Rubar is described by                   : [SOURCE: Page_50]

- the inflow from tributaries : node input; - the inflow from the hillslopes : distributed lateral inflow. The code had to be adapted to handle large lateral inflow files, including 7 node lateral input -from tributaries- and distributed input on every other element. A specific program was also designed to transform automatically Roof&Pipe output file into RubarBe format files. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Comparing flood routing in the main river in RubarBe and in R&P It must be noted that the main channel is not modelled with the same equations in R&P and OS in RubarBe, but there is also a difference in the geometry. \Mrereas R&P and OS use a simplified geometry (rectangular or trapezoid section), a more accurate geometry was used in RubarBe. However, the roughness coefficients are the same. The results are reasonably close. The role of the difference in geometry could be investigated by a test where Rubar would use exactly the same geometry as R&P. [SOURCE: Page_51]

49 [SOURCE: Page_51]

3. Vühy Rubar feedback is not necessary in R&P Except very local subcritical regimes in the main channel of lsepnica, all of the flow is supercritical. There is therefore no influence from the water levels in the lsepnica on the flow in the tributaries, and therefore there is no need to make a two-way linkage. This would indeed involve much more work, because the result of RubarBe in the main channel should be re-injected in the hydrological model at each time-step. [SOURCE: Page_52]

### 4. Modulariÿ as a perspective   :

The linkage was proposed because the hydrological model first tested, OneSecond, had really long computation times : one week on a PC. OneSecond and Roof&Pipe are so far rigid, and any modification of geometry requires a delicate operation to hand-edit geometry files. Besides, to test the impact of adding or suppressing a structure we had to run the whole simulation each time. It was therefore suggested to work in a more modular way: the simulation eould be run separately for each sub-catchment, and injected as lateral input in a hydraulic model of the river. To have the results on the whole catchment, with the chosen repartition of structures, you just needed to select existing files for each of the sub-catchment already computed, and run the hydrologic model only if necessary for the subcatchments where you test a new hypothesis. These scenarios are interesting to study the effect of the spatial repartition on mitigation by desynchronisation of peaks arrival, or if you have to reduce the number of structures to reduce the costs. The linkage makes it also easier to simulate scenarios with heterogeneously distributed rainfall, since the hydrological model so far only work with a homogeneous rainfall. However, this idea was not fully exploited here, because rainfall heterogeneiÿ patterns are not known, and because the transfer times are too short to really make desynchronisation possible. [SOURCE: Page_52]

### 5. Computations that R&P and OS can not handle       :

The combined use of a hydrologic and hydraulic model also makes it possible to handle objects that hydrologic models can not model : . Dry reservoirs . Location of hydraulic jumps to check for bed stability. These examples will be presented and commented in next part. [SOURCE: Page_52]

Figure 12 : Propagation of a flood using input from R&P ; effect of a dry reseryoir Figure 12 immediately shows one of the interests of the linkage: instead of modelling the propagation of one design hydrograph, the linked option makes it possible to actually see the hydrograph grow with the lateral inputs, here from abscissa 142 to abscissa 1310 (red line). The presence of the dry reservoir mitigates the flood downstream, as seen at abscissa 1501 (blue line). \Mth this first draft of the outlet dimensions, 1m x 1m, outlet capaciÿ is about 7 m^3/s. The reservoir is filled after 2000s, and the overflow begins spilling water, as shown by the brutalchange in discharge slope. Abscissa 1418 is located in the reservoir, and the presence of a stored volume will contribute to mitigate the flood curve, even if it is behind the dam (green line) [SOURCE: Page_53]

4.2. Effects of the dry resen oar combined with hillslope structures The results displayed in Fig. 12 show a^25o/o reduction of the peak flow. This result is inferior to our set objective. We will now introduce retention works in the hillslope. We begin by works in the upper part of the catchment, on the road labelled "droga^4" and around it ; the scenario was therefore called "droga^4". Fig. 13 displays the computed discharge at abscissis 1501, first without any hillslope structures (red line, same as curve in Fig. 12), then with "droga^4" works taken into account (blue line), and then with a scenario where all the tributaries discharges are modified (green line). We now see that hillslope works do complement the effect of the dry reservoir. The combination of both types make it possible to reduce the peak from 33 to 12 m^3/s. The effect of the hillslope works [SOURCE: Page_53]

51 [SOURCE: Page_53]

do diminish the time during which the spillway is active, but there is still overflowing. Paper 3 will address the problem of overflow limitation by modiÿing the outlet dimensions. [SOURCE: Page_54]

4.3. Hydraulic jumps Figure 15 displays the computed water level in the dry reservoir neighbourhood (blue line), the velocity (yellow line) and the flow regime as estimated by the Froude number value (two'values green dotted line). \/Vtren the regime changes to supercritical to subcritical, as it will happen upstream the water body behind the dam, a hydraulic jump is expected. It must be noted that the influence of the reservoir evolves with time : the more it fills up, the longer the subcritical regime behind the dam : and therefore the hydraulic jump moves upstreams. The hydraulic modelling will enable us to delimitate the length of the reach where hydraulic jumps can happen, in order to envisage anti-erosion protection measures. [SOURCE: Page_55]

4. Conclusions The first in Poland approach to the slowing down methods with numerical results of their effects has shown that: .    There are important storage possibilities in the mountainous catchment, provided they are located with carefulfield visits. [SOURCE: Page_55]

53 [SOURCE: Page_55]

a    The local effects of the concentrated surface runoff decrease is significant and can result in reduction of landslides and erosion hazards. a   The impact of slowing down structures located in a catchment on flood wave in the main channel is significant, the decrease and flattening of the waves peaks could reduce the use of heavy hydraulic structures in the mountain torrents. a   The land development policy should include catchment management, taking into account the environmental and landscape protection. a   The numerical results show a very good mitigation, but the exact value is still subject to criticism because of the lack of field data. The authors therefore hope for local and national support for a field measurement campaign (rainfall distribution, runoff velocities, stream discharges and discharge-level rating curves... ). a   The construction of "pilot structures" to check their actual behaviour during floods, and monitoring would be desirable. [SOURCE: Page_56]

UTILIZATION OF GREEN ROOF FOR STORING RAINFALL WATER IN URBAN AREAS UTILISER LES IOIIS YERIS EN MILIEU URBAIN POUR SIOCKER LES EAUX DE PLUIE [SOURCE: Page_58]

ZASTOSOWANIE « ZTELONEGO DACHU » W RETENCJONOWANIU OPADU W OBSZARACH LIEJSKICH Wanda Kowalska, Edyta Dro^2dàal Cracow Universiÿ of Technology lnstitute of Water Engineering and Water Management ul. Warszawska 24 31-155 Krakôw tel. [PHONE_REDACTED] w kow al ska@ sm o k. w i s. p k. e d u. p I e drozdzal@ smok.wis. pk.edu. ol Abstract A mathematical model describing individual processes that take place after rainfall occurs on the surface of so called green roof is presented in the paper. The method for solving model equations is given. The advantage of tracing the course of the processes gives the basis for answering the question of how green roofs meet their expected storage capaciÿ demands. An example of model application is presented. Calculations were made for real data and the results were discussed. Résumé L'afticle présente un modèle mathématique décrivanf /e processus pafticulier après les précipitations la suiace appelé « le toit veft »». La méthode pour solutionner les équations du modèle est proposée. L'avantage de tracer le cours des processus donne la base pour répondre à la question de la façon dont "les toits vefts" satisfont /eurs besoins prévus de capacité de stockage. Un exemple de l'application du modèle est présenté. Des calculs ont été effectués pour de vraies données et les résultats ont été discufés. Uzyskujqc moZliwo§é §ledzenia przebiegu wspomnianych procesôw w czasie, stwonono podstawy do odpowiedzi na pytanie, w jakim stopniu zielone dachy spelniajqoczekiwanqod nich retencyjnq rolÇ. 56 [SOURCE: Page_58]

1. lntroduction [SOURCE: Page_59]

Decreased storage capaciÿ of river catchments is one out of many essential Éuses of flood event intensification. This process happens particularly in urban areas. Sealing of vast areas of such surfaces results in the fact that rainwater infiltrates the soil. A considerable part of this amount of water is conveyed through the sewerage system into the river. During intensive rainfalls, fast water outflow generates flood hazard to the areas below the outlet of the sewerage system. Also, as sewers are frequently overfilled, local flooding of buildings and streets occurs frequently. From among the effective methods for increasing water storage capaciÿ at the place where rainfall occurs, cultivating various plants on terraces, balconies and building roofs is a method possible to apply for larger sets of buildings. This ÿpe structures are called green roofs. lnterception and evaporation during rainfall causes that a part of water amount is stopped and the rest is delayed in its way to the surrounding ground or is drained into the sewerage system. Apart from its storage properties, the green cover has also aesthetic values and allows to maintain better air humidiÿ and attenuates the noise. The green roofu in the area of residence or place of employment ensure relax and rest. Such structures are built mainly in Western countries, including Germany, where they are of park and recreational character. ln Poland, despite their high costs and lack of national guidelines and design standards, the possibiliÿ of constructing green roofs is considered more and more frequently. [SOURCE: Page_59]

### 2. Basic constructional components of green roof

The green roof is a multiJayers structure (Fig.1). lts basic components are: a   waterproof layer, thermal insulation, a   draining layer a   vegetation layer. [SOURCE: Page_59]

The waterproof layer should be resistant to failures caused by plant roots, and to chemicals, e.9., fertilizers, and also be completely resistant biologically to mildew and fungi. lt should be resistant to microorganisms and humus acids. The draining layer serves the purposes of constant and full water draining from the vegetation layer and roof surface. The layer is usually made from washed gravel of the 8/16 mm grain or fine aggregate, e.g. keramsite, which has good draining properties. [SOURCE: Page_59]

57 [SOURCE: Page_59]

jÿ! " y'3i.     f t' rainfall r'*§"§ [SOURCE: Page_60]

Vegetation hyer [SOURCE: Page_60]

Thermal insulation [SOURCE: Page_60]

Concreb base [SOURCE: Page_60]

Fig.l. Green roof layers [SOURCE: Page_60]

Water storing mats with hollows with waterproof walls (Fig.2) are also utilized. Excess of water collected in the hollows is drained by a system of channels in between the external walls of the hollows. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Vegetation Vegetation layer [SOURCE: Page_60]

Draining layer [SOURCE: Page_60]

Thermal insulation [SOURCE: Page_60]

Concrete base [SOURCE: Page_60]

Fig-2. Green roof components [SOURCE: Page_60]

The protecting layer isolates the draining layer from the waterproof layer or - depending on the roof construction system - from thepolypropylene thermal insulation layer. The protecting layer (interlayer) is usually made of a               geotextile. ln most cases, the vegetation layer consists of humus mixed with a material of [SOURCE: Page_60]

58 [SOURCE: Page_60]

mineral origin. The grain size of this layer should ensure it the stable structure, which enables water excess to be released to the draining layer. The vegetation layer thickness should range from 9 to 15 cm for extensive cultivation, to 35 cm for intensive low cultivation, and from 35 to 250 cm for intensive high cultivation. The vegetation and draining layers are separated by a filtration layer from a geotextile preventing small soil particles from entering the draining layer, protecting it against silting. As it was mentioned above, taking into consideration the ÿpe of vegetation used to green roof construction, two ÿpes of green roof can be distinguished: .  intensive green rooh, .  extensive green roofs. For extensive cultivation, low plants are used with low demands. This plants, e.g. steppe plants, can live and grow with no special care. lntensive cultivation means roof gardens with lawns, bushes and trees. The plants used there have high demands concerning their care and require regular feeding with water and nutrients. The choice of the ÿpe of vegetation cover depends, first of all, on the roof stabiliÿ and strength, and also on its tilt. Extensively cultivated green roofs are most frequent as their construction and maintenance are not too expensive. lf such a vegetation cover is used, the green roof construction is thin-layered. lntensive cultivation requires careful selection of material for individual layers; also plant watering should be then planned. [SOURCE: Page_61]

3. Mathematical model of rainwater seepage through the green roof layers To describe hydrological processes occurring on a green roof after rainfall occurrence, the amounts of interception and evapotranspiration are determined, then net rainfall is transformed into runoff. The latter process was described with two models. [SOURCE: Page_61]

A. Mathematical model of rainwater seepage in the vegetation layer [SOURCE: Page_61]

Formulating the model, an assumption was accepted that water movement in the vegetation layer is an unsteady onedimensional vertical movement. Flow parameters depend on time f and distance z. Rain intensiÿ course in time f is represented by rain intensiÿ values determined for specified time intervals À/ (Fig.3). The Green-Ampt model was employed to describe water movement. lt is adopted here that each change in rain intensiÿÂnd, =nd,-nd,-r , i=1,2,...i^1 generates a new, Êth wetting front of constant moisture O, in the area between the soil surface and the first location of the generated front (see Fig.4). All wetting fronts generated in successive time intervals travel down the vegetation layer with a constant velociÿ uf,, characteristic for a given front, reaching locations zfu) at [SOURCE: Page_61]

59 [SOURCE: Page_61]

successive time instantr 1(") - I,2,...,1i. The process continues until the water collected in the soil volume bounded from below by the wetting front is drained from the vegetation layer. ln time intervals with constant rain intensiÿ (interval [rt'l,rrrl] in Fig.3), single rain intensiÿ change (in Fig.3: Ntd, attime instant /(0)) generates one wetting front. At the time the Èth front traveling down the vegetation layer reaches its bottom, water outflow will occur at rate specified by equation (2). The outflow rate will change after next front reaches the bottom of the layer. [SOURCE: Page_65]

B. Mathematical model of rainwater seepage in the draining layer [SOURCE: Page_65]

To determine unsteady water flow in the draining layer being the saturation zone, Boussinesq equation, twodimensional in horizontal plane, was adopted: a             àH.       a         AH           AH ( h kn         +      (h k^7, -:- )+w = 11                 (e) àx             ^) dx        ày         ày          àt where: p- storage coefficient (effective porosiÿ), [-] w - infiltration recharge (from vegetation layer), [m/s] h - hydraulic depth of the aquifer, [m] kn -coelficient of water permeabiliÿ, [m/s] H- hydraulic head, [m]. The equation above is a nonlinear second-order partial parabolic differential equation. To obtain a unique solution of equation (9), it is necessary to speciÿ the initial condition and boundary conditions. Together with adopted conditions, equation (9) constitutes an initial-boundary problem with unique solution. The domain where the solution of equation (9) is sought is a rectangle Ltc x Ly with its sides measured along the x and y axes, respectively, of the Cartesian co-ordinate system with its origin at the lower left vertex of the mentioned rectangle (see Fig. 5). This rectangle is the base of a rectangular prism cut of the green root whose three lateral faces are impermeable while in the middle of the fourth face, at its base, a rectangular outflow opening of width Zw and height hu' is located. The rectangular prism is filled with gravel up to the height, mz , overlaid with the vegetation layer. [SOURCE: Page_65]

### 4. Calculation example

Four calculation examples were considered. ln the first one, it was assumed that the roof was covered with sheet metal only, in the second and third, a gravel layer on the roof was taken into account, in the fourth case rainwater flow through vegetation and draining layers on the roof was considered. [SOURCE: Page_67]

It was assumed that the area of rainwater flow within the roof surface is of rectangular prism shape divided along roof length into sections of identical dimensions. Carrying out calculations for the first case, it was assumed that rainwater flows down from the roof on the whole its width. Onedimensional [SOURCE: Page_67]

65 [SOURCE: Page_67]

kinematic wave equation was used to model the surface flow. Two ways of water draining from a gravel-covered roof were analyzed: along all the roof width (case 2) or through the opening located in the middle part of the front wall of each section (see Fig. 5), at its bottom base (case 3). The rainwater flow over the roof surface was in both cases described by the Boussinesq equation, one-dimensional in case 2, two-dimensional in case 3. When calculating rainwater flow through vegetation and draining layers of the green roof (case 4), mathematical model described in detail in Section 3 was applied assuming as in case 3 that water is drained off the roof by the opening located in the middle part of the front wall of each section. [SOURCE: Page_68]

All calculations were made for individual roof section of l^0 mx 4.45 m x 0.19 m . The obtained results are repeatable for each section. The width and height of the opening in the middle part of the front wall of the section were 0.15 m and 0.1m, respectively. When gravel layer was considered on the roof, it was assumed that this was a homogeneous layer of constant thickness mz=0.1m with coefficient of water permeabiliÿ fut=I00 mlday. lf over the gravel layer being a draining layer a vegetation layer was located, it was assumed that its thickness mt =0.09m, it was made of peat with coefficients lcn,a,n ate 0.15, 3.5 and 0.85, respectively. Calculations were made for peat initial moisture equal to the plant permanent wilting point @o . [SOURCE: Page_68]

Calculation for an individual roof section were carried out on a rectangular net spread over the roof with Lx x Ly = 0.25mx^0.05m mesh. Time step Âf = 60s was used in calculation made for the vegetation layer while the time step assumed for the sheet-metal roof and gravel layer was smaller (Ât = 6s) which was caused by the stabiliÿ requirements of the used difference schemes. [SOURCE: Page_68]

ln the first three calculation cases, it was assumed the rainfall intensiÿ z(f) n constant and its duration is as in Fig. 8. The results of calculations made for these cases are shown in Figs. 9 through 14 as input rainwater hydrographs and the hydrographs of rainwater released from the roof, and as time courses of input and output water volumes. [SOURCE: Page_68]

### 5. Concluding remarks

It can be stated that a green roof of construction described in this paper meets effectively the expected storage demands. Such a structure intercepts precipitation water and substantially lengthens the time of its outflow, reducing at the same time to some extent water volume. The results of the calculation example presented above are a good illustration of this statement. lt should also be stressed that the basis for the calculations done above included an innovative numerical model of green roof; it seems that it is both reliable and effective model. It is certain that further research work is purposeful, both as the work on theoretical description of functioning of green roofs, and the work on design solutions. This results from the experience of implementations made, assessed with changing climatic conditions and dynamic progress of urbanization taken into account, which frequently generates flood hazard. A green roof is a structure that, if widely utilized in urban areas, can substantially mitigate this hazard. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Modeling of River Bed Deformations [SOURCE: Page_77]

What problems can be solved by a 1-D river sediment transport model? An example of RubarBE software Quels problèmes résoudre par un code lD de transport de sédiments en rivière? Exemple du logiciel RubarBE Mo^2liwo§ci zastosowania j ednowymiarowego modelu matematy czfiego transportu rumowi sk a. P rryklad: Rub arb e software [SOURCE: Page_78]

A. Paquier Cemagref, Hydrology and Hydraulics Research Unit, 3 bis Quai Chauveau, CP220, 69336 Lyon Cedex 09, France, Tel: +33 47220 8611, Fax: +33 4 78 47 7875, andre.paquier@ cemagref .fr [SOURCE: Page_78]

English abstract With a 1-D sediment transport model, various problems can be investigated. The most obvious one is to estimate the amount of sediment transported, which can be found by balancing inputs, outputs and exchanges with the river bed. Sediment transport during floods can be high and, particularly in mountain rivers, one flood may significantly alter the morphology of the river bed. lt implies that the model should integrate uneven flow and be able to describe the changes to the shape of the cross sections. Cemagref developed RubarBE to include these two last aspects, concentrating on one single type of sediment found in the bed material load. An example of application of RubarBE to the River lsère is provided. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Résumé français Avec un modèle unidimensionnel de transpori de sédiments, plusieurs problèmes peuvent être approchés. Le plus évident est l'estimation de la quantité de sédiments transportés en faisant le bilan entre appofts, sorties et échanges avec le fond. Un fort transport de sédiments se produit pendant /es crues et, particulièrement en rivières de montagne, une crue peut modifier la morphologie d'un lit de rivière notablement. Ceci implique que le modèle doit intégrer le régime transitoire et pouvoir décrire l'évolution de la forme des sections en travers. Le Cemagref a développé RubarBE pour inclure ces deux derniers aspects en se concentrant sur un seul ÿpe de sédiments supposé constituer le matériau du lit. Un exemple d'application de Rubar BE à l'lsère est présenté. [SOURCE: Page_78]

75 [SOURCE: Page_78]

Streszczenie polskie Przepÿw w korycie cieku opisywany iest zwykle iednowymiarowym modelem matematycznym. Jednym z podstawowych zastosowa tego typu modelu, iest mo liwo okre lania ilo ci transportowanego rumowiska oraz zwi zanych z ni zmian koryta w profilu podlu nym. Podczas przepÿwu wÔd powodziowych wyst puje intensywny ruch rumowiska wleczonego. Cz sto dochodzi do znacznei zmiany jego granulacji., czego nast pstwem jest wyst pienia du ych zmian w konfiguracji dna Rubarbe umo liwia obliczenia w warunkach ruchu zmiennego opisuj c jednocze nie zmiany w przekroiach poprzecznych. Program Rubarbe powstal w instytucie badawczym Cemagref. Przykladem zastosowania programu Rubarbe s obliczenia prowadzone na rzece lsere. [SOURCE: Page_79]

1. lntroduction Sediment transport is one of the main process that should be considered in studying "natural" rivers. Even if the sediment load is low, exchanges are always occuring between the banks, the bottom and the flow. This process, that is at the origin of the morphology of the rivers, is still acting and may have huge consequences on human activities or the environment. This paper recalls the problems that may appear and the main processes and parameters that are concerned. Then the sediment transport software RubarBE is described and its use on the River lsere is detailed. [SOURCE: Page_79]

2. The questions linked with sediment transport Except the cases of the very concentrated sediment loads that change the properties of water, the main questions concern the evolution of the depositions or the erosions that occur along the river. lf the problem is integrated for a long period, the volume of sediment for one stretch of river has to be assessed. More generally, the question of the evolution of the bed and the banks at any moment, and its effects on the flow features, should be answered. Typical problems can be emphasized. Flood risk is directly linked to the level of water in the river in relation to the height of the banks. Of course, the presence of sediment deposits can raise the water level, but sediment transport may also change the interactions between the flow and the bed, thus modifying the water level. One example is the appearance of dunes in sandy rivers, which develop due to increasing sediment loads. ln any case, whatever the size of the sediments concerned, the assessment of flood risk can be altered strongly and the hazard increase can lead to unexpected damages. However, an intense erosion that can reduce the flood hazard can lead to the destruction of the structures (bridges, weirs, etc) that cross the river. Environmental impacts are also often linked with the transport of fine sediments that are likely to carry pollutants (heavy metals, pesticides, etc). Moreover, by itself, the deposition of fine sediments in areas with gravel bed leads to a decrease of the ecological potentialities. [SOURCE: Page_79]

76 [SOURCE: Page_79]

3. Basic requirements of a 1-D sediment transport model Most part of the sediment transport occurs during high flows. The flood events should be modelled in a detailed way to assess the main morphological changes. This fact implies that uneven flow modelling should be possible. This unevenness may be connected with the characteristics of the flow linked with the river basin, but also with the evolution of the bed during the flood itself. Coupling of sediment transport modelling and flow modelling are thus necessary, although for engineering studies, the approximation of steady flow can be often used if the flood events last a long time and the bottom evolution is very slow. This point is also due to the difficulties of calibrating a sediment transport model that generally relies on measurements taken before and after the flood event only. Development of technical devices such as the ones that allow bathymetry measurements during the flood event could be a relevant means to increase accuracy of calibration for very transient processes. The processes within the river itself could then be followed with both small space and time steps. However, except for solving local questions (for instance around a structure), the main questions remain at the scale of the whole reach of a river (conesponding to the scale of the river basin) for which the river flow can be described by a 1-D model. lt implies that, in most cases, a 1-D sediment transport model is the most suitable; it takes into account a smaller set of parameters, which also means simpler calibration. The parameters specific to sediment transport models should describe both the way of transportation, and the exchange fluxes between the bottom (including the banks) and the flow. The basic distinctions are between bed material load and suspended material load, although in most cases, the models will consider the two separately. The main factor of the bed material load is the sediment transport capaciÿ that will define the maximum sediment discharge that can be transported by the flow in uniform conditions. The methods of assessment of this capaciÿ come from empirical relations that take into account the flow parameters (water depth, velociÿ, etc) and the sediment parameters (diameters of the various groups, interactions between these groups, etc). However, the main parameters are certainly the bottom shear stress (determined from the flow parameters), the critical shear stress under which no transport will occur (it is assessed from both features of individual sediments), and the organisation of sediments in the bed of the river. The last parameter (specific to 1-D models) is the active width, which is a multiplying factor of the unit sediment discharge, and it corresponds to the width of the cross section on which the transport of sediments is effective. For the wash load, the interactions with the bottom are very weak and the main parameter is the fall velociÿ that determines it relative to the flow behaviour (particularly turbulence), the sediment particle will be deposited. [SOURCE: Page_80]

77 [SOURCE: Page_80]

4. Description of RubarBE software RubarBE is a onedimensional sediment transport model that tends to represent the uneven flow and the bed evolution in the case of a bed material load. The set of relations used in the model is as follows: [SOURCE: Page_81]

De Saint Venant equations for water: [SOURCE: Page_81]
