183 [SOURCE: Page_187]

3. Observed problems and threats ln the time of flood of 1997, with culmination at about flow of Qzy", there was a threat of mass overtopping of dikes in some areas, what was especially dangerous when the degree and ÿpe of urban development is concerned (fig 1). Excessive ftltration across body and foundations of a dike was observed in many places. Those places were temporarily protected with sand bags. Lush vegetation in between the dikes caused the increase in flow resistance, what in turn made the actual level of water much higher, Single trees growing on (or very near) the slopes of dikes caused flow disturbances and increased erosion of slopes, as well as washing away of dike foundations. Trees carried by the river were blocking flow beneath the bridges, directly threatening them as well. The technical assessment done just after the flood confirmed that conditions of the dikes and accompanying objects required performing detailed studies and assessments, which then could be used for developing projects for their modernization. The scope and cost of the undertaking were the reason that several stages of works were assumed. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Figure 2= Area between flood dikes, Sola river in the ciÿ of O§wiçcim [SOURCE: Page_188]

184 [SOURCE: Page_188]

4. Geotechnical analysis The main purpose of the geotechnical analysis was to: a) determine ÿpe of soils used for construction of dikes and in their foundations, b) assess the geotechnical parameters of that soils, concerning filtration and stability of dikes. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Analysis was done in cross sections of about 100 meters apart. There were three openings for each cross section (see below), up to 4 meters below dike foundations. [SOURCE: Page_189]

No   1             No$_2$              No$_3$ Dike crest 1:2                   2. [SOURCE: Page_189]

\ [SOURCE: Page_189]

Figure 3: Placement of research openings in a dike cross section [SOURCE: Page_189]

Compaction analysis was performed for body of a dike (the central opening - No 2). [SOURCE: Page_189]

Assessment of results for the analysis leads to the following conclusions: , dikes are made of heterogenous soils -   cohesive soils: clay and silt (loam) - non-cohesive soils: medium grain sands, gravel and sand with dust interlayers à    cohesive soils: hard and plastic state, degree of plasticiÿ of l^1=9.1 lo 0.42 compaction degree ls>O.92 ,    non-cohesive soils are in loose state, compaction degree of 16=9.13 to 0.35 ,    dike foundations are changeable. Some sections of dusts (layers height of 1 to 2.5 meters) then sands, or only sands just beneath the dikes. Third type of foundations are transition forms between cohesive and non- cohesive soils. [SOURCE: Page_189]

5. Filtration and stability analysis Parameters of the dikes change along their lenght: crest width is 3 to 6 meters, slope inclination is 1:2to 1:2.5 (but locally it may be 1:1.5 and 1:4), height 2to 4 meters. Numerical analysis of filtration and stabiliÿ, which took into consideration parameters of dikes and flood flow lead to the following conclusions: - There is no established filtration across bodies of dikes made of cohesive soils. -   There is a risk of material washout and piping in dikes made of non-cohesive soils (sands) and of layered structure. -   There is a real risk of stabiliÿ failure for dikes with slope inclination of 1:1.5, regardless of ÿpe of material used for construction. [SOURCE: Page_191]

6. Technical assessment of dikes and modernization quidelines Final technical assessment of dikes that was formulated was based on research, analysis and assessments. Formulated modernization quidelines adressed all mentioned aspects, as well as for local conditions. So: )   Hydrological analysis of the flood flow transformation took into consideration interactions among flows in different rivers. lt showed that existing vegetation in beteen the dikes increases the expected water level of about 40cm (when compared with vegetation-free state). lf vegetation (especially trees) is allowed to grow, this limitation can be further increased, with direct impact on the required dike height. There is a project for protection of forest and vegetation of outstanding value in that area, proposed by non-govermental environment protection organisations (fig. 2). !n this project all activiÿ towards removal of trees is very much reduced. An agreement was made with those organisations, in which removing of trees was indeed considerably limited. For such flow conditions calculations were made for the required (safe) dike height, with nominal river flow Q.=Qo.zyo. As a result of that decisions and calculations all dikes in the area of interest need to be made higher-of 1.8 meteror less. )   Geotechnical and filtration analysis, together with data collected directly during the flood showed that dike tightness should be increased, what leads to improvement of slope stabiliÿ. Considerable raising of crest level of dikes had to be very closely connected with increasing their tightness. Compaction level of soils in the dikes was low with direct impact on their permeabiliÿ. Composition of soils in the dikes and in their foundations were changeable. The best option was to rise the crest level from the river side, using cohesive soils. lt was also a way to stop further degradation of dikes made of non-cohesive or layered soils. [SOURCE: Page_191]

187 [SOURCE: Page_191]

t   Observations made directly during flood, as well as data coming from analysis and technical assessments showed that much attention had to be given to: -    dike sections based on permeable and layered soils, -    dike culverts, -   removal of trees which are a threat to dike safeÿ on existing dike slopes (or special means of protection should be developed), -   connections to roads and bridges, with special emphasis on safe height and limitations for bridge heights, -   end sections of dikes on main river and on tributaries. [SOURCE: Page_192]

7. lnitial projects for coal mine deads utilization As it was shown in point 6 all requriments could be easily met if materials used comprise of cohesive soils. As there was not enough such soils in between dikes and because of economic reasons the use of mine deads from nearby mines was allowed. Detailed composition of the deads is presented in table 4. One of the characteristic features of mine deads is their suceptibiliÿ to mechanical crushing and weathering. Changing geotechnical parameters along with the parameters mentioned above are presented in tables 1 and^2 and in fig. 5 - for mines materials from which they were taken. Utilization of such materialwas met with reservations of ecological organisations. Qualiÿ analysis did not confirm adverse impact of those materials on the environment. [SOURCE: Page_192]

8. Stages of works The main criterium for dividing the entire scope of works into stages was connected with longitudal changes of safeÿ level along dikes (not sufficient height, high permeabiliÿ). Availiabiliÿ of funds was also a factor. Therefore the scope of works was divided into several stages. lt applies for both the height and length of a dike. [SOURCE: Page_195]

Second stage [SOURCE: Page_195]

First stage Existing dike                                 V [SOURCE: Page_195]

Figure 9: Works stages for dikes modemization, cross section [SOURCE: Page_195]

Bad condition [SOURCE: Page_195]

Very bad                    Very bad condition condition [SOURCE: Page_195]

Bad condition                              Bad condition [SOURCE: Page_195]

Figure 10. Works stages, considering as first bad technical condition of sections of dikes [SOURCE: Page_195]

\Mren the safeÿ of the region is concerned, longitudinal division should be considered first. For the first stage lowest and the most permeable dikes were to be selected. For the second stage - medium compacted dikes, for the third stage: dike sections made of cohesive soils (tight) It must be clearly stated that beyond these general guidelines many local problems had to be considered. [SOURCE: Page_195]

9. Conclusion 1. Floods which occuned in recent years damaged many sections of dikes, and showed that their technical condition is bad and they had to be urgently modernized. [SOURCE: Page_195]

191 [SOURCE: Page_195]

2. One of the main issues for effective dike modernization planning was to comprehend historical and current data concerning their composition and actual operation. lt is therefore important to create a database concerning the dikes. Therefore it is important to create a database of the dikes comprising information on technical conditions and performance of dikes in a GIS computer format, with abiliÿ to assessment of technical condition of a given dike section, planned repair and modernization works, identification of possible threats. This application should be able to use digital tenain models, digital maps and hydraulical models used for flood flow transformation analysis. A SIRS programme, under development by CEMAGREF could be a good example for that. There is a work in progress to adapt that programme to Poland - made in collaboration between CEMAGREF and llGW PK. [SOURCE: Page_196]

3. Safeÿ level of the areas protected by dikes depend on the ÿpe and level of urban development in those areas. The height of dikes is determined by calculated water level for the selected nominal flows. This problem has not been solved in Polish technical guidelines. ln different technical solutions the problem is dealt with in a different way. [SOURCE: Page_196]

4. Flood protection dikes can be modernized using materials coming from mine deads, on a condition, however, that strict technical requirements for their utilization must be observed. lt applies both to the construction and further use of dikes. Dikes which were built in the past with these materials did not meet permeabiliÿ requirements, even when cross sections dimensions had been increased. [SOURCE: Page_196]
